An inverse source problem for convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations with the final overdetermination 1,2 Department of Mathematics, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee-IIT Roorkee, Haridwar Highway, Roorkee, Uttarakhand 247667, INDIA. e-mail: Pardeep Kumar: pkumar3@ma.iitr.ac.in. e-mail: Manil T. Mohan: manilfma@iitr.ac.in, maniltmohan@gmail.com. * Corresponding author. Key words: Convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations, inverse problem, final overdetermination, Tikhonov’s fixed point theorem. Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): Primary 35R30; Secondary 35Q35, 35Q30.
Pardeep Kumar 1 and Manil T. Mohan 2*

Abstract
. In this paper, we examine an inverse problem for the following convective Brinkman-Forchheimer (CBF) equations or damped Navier-Stokes equations: 𝒗 t − μ Δ 𝒗 + ( 𝒗 ⋅ ∇ ) 𝒗 + α 𝒗 + β | 𝒗 | r − 1 𝒗 + ∇ p = 𝑭 := 𝒇 g , ∇ ⋅ 𝒗 = 0 , \displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla p=\boldsymbol{F}:=\boldsymbol{f}g,\ \ \ \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0, on a torus 𝕋 d \mathbb{T}^{d} , d = 2 , 3 d=2,3 . The inverse problem under consideration consists of determining the vector-valued velocity function 𝒗 \boldsymbol{v} , the pressure gradient ∇ p \nabla p and the vector-valued forcing function 𝒇 \boldsymbol{f} . Using the Tikhonov fixed point theorem, we prove the existence of a solution for the inverse problem for 2D and 3D CBF equations with the final overdetermination data for the divergence free initial data in the energy space 𝕃 2 ​ ( 𝕋 d ) \mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}) . A concrete example is also provided to validate the obtained result. Moreover, we overcome the technical difficulties while proving the uniqueness and Hölder type stability results by using the regularity results available for the direct problem for CBF equations. The well-posedness results hold for r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 in two dimensions and for r ≥ 3 r\geq 3 in three dimensions for appropriate values of α , μ \alpha,\mu and β \beta . The nonlinear damping term | 𝒗 | r − 1 ​ 𝒗 |\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v} plays a crucial role in obtaining the required results. In the case of supercritical growth ( r > 3 r>3 ), we obtain better results than that are available in the literature for 2D Navier-Stokes equations.



1.
Introduction

The convective Brinkman-Forchheimer (CBF) equations characterize the motion of incomp-ressible fluid flows in a saturated porous medium (cf.
[
5
,
12
]
).
The major objective of this work is to examine the well-posedness of an inverse problem to CBF equations with periodic boundary conditions for the divergence free initial data in the energy space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

1.1.
The mathematical model and the direct problem

Let
L>0
and
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/(L\mathbb{Z})^{d}\cong(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d},

d=2,3
, be the
d
-dimensional torus. We consider the following CBF equations on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla p=\boldsymbol{F}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{f}g,\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T),

(1.2)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \ \ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T),

with the initial condition

(1.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times\{0\},

and
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
p(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfy the following periodicity conditions:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(x+{L}e_{i},t)=\boldsymbol{v}(x,t),\ \ \text{and}\ \ p(x+{L}e_{i},t)=p(x,t),\ \text{ for all }\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T],

for
i=1,\ldots,d
, where
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\}
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
represents the velocity field,
p(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the pressure field and
\boldsymbol{F}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
stands for an external force which is periodic in the first variable, that is,
\boldsymbol{F}(x+{L}e_{i},t)=\boldsymbol{F}(x,t),\text{ for all }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
. The constant
\mu
denotes the positive Brinkman coefficient (effective viscosity), while the positive constants
\alpha
and
\beta
stand for the Darcy coefficient (permeability of the porous medium) and the Forchheimer coefficient (proportional to the porosity of the material), respectively (cf.
[
17
,
34
]
). The parameter
r\in[1,\infty)
is known as the absorption exponent and the cases,
r=3
and
r>3
, are referred as the critical exponent and the fast growing nonlinearity, respectively (see
[
27
]
). When
\alpha=\beta=0
, the classical
d
-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations (NSE) are obtained. Thus, the system (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) can be viewed as a modification (by the introduction of an absorption term
\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
) of the classical NSE (or damped Navier-Stokes equations), and the damping term helps to obtain global solvability results even in three dimensions. By imposing the condition
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p(x,t)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=0,
for
t\in[0,T]
, one can obtain the uniqueness of the pressure
p
. The model given in (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) is recognized to be more accurate when the flow velocity is too large for the Darcy’s law to be valid alone, and apart from that, the porosity is not too small, thus, we call these types of models as
non-Darcy models
(cf.
[
34
]
). In Proposition 1.1,
[
19
]
, it is demonstrated that the critical homogeneous CBF equations have the same scaling as NSE only when
\alpha=0
and no scale invariance property for other values of
\alpha
and
r
.

The existence and uniqueness of weak solutions satisfying the energy equality and strong solutions for CBF equations in bounded and periodic domains is established in the works
[
1
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
27
,
28
,
35
]
, etc., and references therein, and for the whole space, the results can be accessed from
[
6
,
50
]
, etc. The Navier-Stokes problem with a modified absorption term
|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
, for
r>1
, in bounded domains with compact boundary is considered in
[
1
]
. The existence of Leray-Hopf weak solutions, for any dimension
d\geq 2
, and its uniqueness for
d=2
is established in
[
1
]
. In
[
19
]
, the authors obtained a simple proof of the existence of global-in-time smooth solutions of 3D CBF equations in periodic domains with the absorption exponent
r>3
. For the critical value
r=3
, the existence of a unique global, regular solution is proved, provided that the coefficients satisfy a relation
4\beta\mu\geq 1
. The authors in
[
20
]
proved that the strong solutions of 3D CBF equations in periodic domains with the absorption exponent
r\in[1,3]
remain strong under small changes of the initial condition and forcing function. Recently, for
r\geq 3

(\beta,\mu>0\ \text{for}\ r>3
and
2\beta\mu\geq 1
for
r=3
), the long time behavior of 3D deterministic and stochastic CBF equations defined on a torus is discussed in
[
28
]
.

1.2.
Investigation of the inverse problem

Despite the importance of the direct problem, it necessitates the knowledge of physical parameters such as the Brinkman coefficient
\mu
, Darcy coefficient
\alpha
, Forchheimer coefficient
\beta
and the forcing term
\boldsymbol{F}:=\boldsymbol{f}g
. When, in addition to the solution of the equation, recovery of some physical properties of the investigated object or the effects of external sources are needed, it is better to use inverse problems to determine a coefficient or to handle the right hand side of the differential equation arising in a mathematical model of a physical phenomena. However, posing an inverse problem requires some additional information on the solution besides the given initial and boundary conditions.

In this work, as an additional information, we use the trace of the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
and the pressure gradient
\nabla p
, as prescribed at the final moment
t=T
of the segment
[0,T]
. We assume that
\boldsymbol{F}
, the vector-valued external force in (
1.1
), can be written as

\boldsymbol{F}(x,t):=\boldsymbol{f}(x)g(x,t),

where
\boldsymbol{f}
is an unknown divergence-free vector-valued function and
g
is a given scalar function such that
g
and
g_{t}
are continuous on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T]
. We consider the nonlinear inverse problem of determining the functions
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
, satisfying the system (
1.1
)-(
1.4
), with the final overdetermination condition:

(1.5)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(x,T)=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(x),\ \ \ \ \nabla p(x,T)=\nabla\psi(x),\ \ \ x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

where the functions
\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varphi},\nabla\psi,\mu,\alpha,\beta
and
g
are given. Note that
\boldsymbol{F}
satisfies

(1.6)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{F}(x,t)=0\ \ \text{for all}\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T),

and this condition is required for the uniqueness of determining the spatially varying divergence-free factor
\boldsymbol{f}
of the source term
\boldsymbol{F}(x,t):=\boldsymbol{f}(x)g(x,t)
, where
g
is a given function.

Let us now provide one example to show that the divergence free condition on
\boldsymbol{F}
is necessary for obtaining uniqueness of the inverse problem. Suppose that the condition (
1.6
) is not satisfied. Then, for

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v},\ \ \text{and}\ \ g\equiv 1,

we assume that
(\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla p=\mathbf{0},\ \ \ \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0,

and we fix
\boldsymbol{v}(x,0)
. Under the condition (
1.4
) and
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p(x,t)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=0,
for
t\in[0,T],
it is trivial that
(\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p)=(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})
satisfies (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with
\boldsymbol{f}\equiv\mathbf{0}
. Now, we choose
\psi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfying
\nabla\psi\not\equiv\mathbf{0}
in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Then
(\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p)=(\mathbf{0},\nabla\psi)
satisfies the same system (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with
\boldsymbol{f}=\nabla\psi
. In other words, in the presence of the pressure
p
in the CBF equations, there is no possibility of uniquely determining the component of
\boldsymbol{f}
given by a scalar potential. If the condition (
1.6
) is satisfied, then
\psi\equiv 0
and
\boldsymbol{f}
can be determined uniquely.

Inverse problems with final overdetermination conditions have been well studied in the literature (see
[
15
,
21
,
22
,
24
,
25
,
31
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
45
,
49
]
, etc., and references therein). An inverse problem
with the final overdetermination for NSE has first been considered by A. I. Prilepko and I. A. Vasin in
[
40
]
(cf. Sections 4.2 and 4.3, Chapter 4,
[
42
]
). In the work
[
42
]
, they proved the existence and uniqueness results of an inverse problem for nonstationary linearized NSE with final and integral overdetermination conditions using Schauder’s fixed point theorem. By an application of Schauder’s fixed point theorem, the solvability results of an inverse problem for the nonlinear nonstationary Navier-Stokes systems in the case of final overdetermination is established in
[
41
,
49
]
(cf. Chapter 4,
[
42
]
). However, neither uniqueness nor stability are taken into account for the same problem in the works
[
41
,
49
]
. For an extensive study on numerous inverse problems corresponding to Navier-Stokes equations and related models, where one requires to determine the density of external forces or some coefficients of the equations on the basis of integral or functional overdetermination, we refer the interested readers to
[
4
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
13
,
14
,
23
,
24
,
26
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
33
,
39
,
40
,
42
]
, etc. and references therein.

The simultaneous determination of source terms in a linear parabolic problem from the observation of the final state is achieved through the use of a weak solution approach, as demonstrated in
[
21
]
(cf.
[
22
]
). The authors in
[
15
]
examined the well-posedness of an inverse problem for 2D NSE with the final overdetermination data using the Tikhonov fixed point theorem. To prove the same, they assumed that the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
and the viscosity constant is sufficiently large. Recently, based on the existence of strong solutions of CBF equations, the well-posedness of an inverse problem for 2D and 3D CBF equations with the final overdetermination data is established in
[
31
]
using Schauder’s fixed point theorem, where the authors assumed that the initial data is sufficiently smooth
(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega)\cap\mathbb{V},\ \mbox{ where }\ \Omega\ \text{is a bounded domain})
. The main difference of our work with the results obtained in
[
31
]
is that we are proving the well-posedness of the final overdetermination problem with
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
under much relaxed conditions than that obtained in
[
15
]
. The nonlinear damping term
|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
helps us to control the convective term
(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}
and achieve the required results. An inverse problem of determining the initial condition for 2D and 3D CBF equations, given direct observations of the time dependent velocity field at a finite set of points at positive times (Eulerian observations) in periodic domains, is examined in
[
37
]
.

1.3.
Technical difficulties and approaches

We emphasize here that the method used in
[
15
]
(for the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
) may only be applicable for the case of
d=2,\ r\in[1,3]
(see
[
32
]
), due to a technical difficulty in working with bounded domains. Note that in the case of bounded domains,
\mathrm{P}_{\mathbb{H}}(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v})
(
\mathrm{P}_{\mathbb{H}}
is the Helmholtz-Hodge orthogonal projection, see Subsection
2.1
) need not be zero on the boundary, and
\mathrm{P}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\Delta
are not necessarily commuting (for a counter example, see Example 2.19,
[
44
]
). Furthermore, while taking the inner product with
-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}
in (
1.1
),
-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\neq 0
on the boundary of the domain (
\boldsymbol{n}
is the outward drawn normal to the boundary
\partial\Omega
), in general and the term with pressure may not disappear (see
[
27
]
). As a result, the equality (
[
19
]
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(x))\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x+4\bigg(\frac{r-1}{(r+1)^{2}}\bigg)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}|^{r}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

(1.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\frac{r-1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-3}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

may not be useful in the context of bounded domains. So, we restrict ourselves to periodic domains in this work, and the equality (
1.3
) plays a crucial role in obtaining the well-posedness of solutions of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
). Recently, the authors in
[
46
]
addressed the above regularity problem for Dirichlet’s boundary conditions and the well-posedness of such kinds of inverse problems for CBF equations in bounded domains will be a future work.

1.4.
Main results and novelties of the work

By a solution of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), we mean a set of vector-valued functions
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
such that

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),\ \nabla p(\cdot,t)\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\ \boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H},

for any
t\in[0,T]
and the triplet
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
satisfies all the relations (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) in the weak sense.
In order to prove the well-posedness of the inverse problem for the CBF equations in
[
31
]
, the authors exploited the existence of unique strong solutions for the direct problem for the CBF equations with smooth initial data (like
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega)\cap\mathbb{V}
). However, in practice, the data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varphi},\nabla\psi
, and
g
that are obtained from physical measurements may not be smooth functions. As a result, the methods based on the assumption of smooth initial data are not applicable in such cases. In this paper, we employ a weak solution approach to formulate and solve the inverse problem for the triplet
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
by using Tikhonov’s fixed point theorem. We employ the methods developed in
[
15
,
41
]
to prove the existence of solutions and
[
15
]
to establish the uniqueness and stability to the above formulated inverse problem. The aim of the present paper is to remove the growth restriction (see
[
32
]
) and verify the well-posedness of solutions of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) with an arbitrary growth exponent for
r\geq 1
, in 2D and for
r\geq 3
, in 3D. Moreover, for the supercritical growth (that is, for
r>3
), the conditions on
\mu
are much weaker than that obtained in
[
15
]
for 2D NSE (cf. Remark
4.2
below). The major goals of this paper is to prove

(i)

the existence of a solution (using Tikhonov’s fixed point theorem) and its uniqueness,

(ii)

the stability of the solution in the norm of the corresponding function spaces,

to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) under the assumptions:

(1.8)

\displaystyle|g(x,T)|\geq g_{T}>0\ \text{ for some positive constant}\ g_{T}\ \text{for}\ x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

and

(1.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H},\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathbb{V},\ \ \ \nabla\psi\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

In contrast to the results obtained for the CBF equations in
[
31
,
32
]
, and
[
15
,
42
]
, etc., for NSE, the well-posedness of the generalized solution of the inverse problem holds for the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
in the periodic domains. Moreover, a Hölder type stability result is also obtained in this work.

We now state the main result on the well-posedness of solutions of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{d}\ (d=2,3)
be the
d
-dimensional torus,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H},\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathbb{V},\ \nabla\psi\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
g,g_{t}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
satisfy assumption (
1.8
). Moreover, let the conditions

(1.10)

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4\alpha^{\frac{1}{3}}}\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}^{\frac{4}{3}}\leq\mu,\ \alpha>0,\ \ \ \text{for}\ d=2\ \text{and}\ r\in[1,3],

(1.11)

\displaystyle\frac{2(r-3)}{\alpha(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{8}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-3}}<\mu,\ \alpha>0,\ \ \ \ \text{for}\ d=2,3\ \text{and}\ r>3,

(1.12)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}<\mu,\ \alpha>0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ d=3\ \text{and}\ r=3,

hold, where
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
be sufficiently large as discussed in Remark
4.2
below. Then, the following assertions hold for the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
):

(i)

There exists a solution
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
).

(ii)

Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{i},\nabla p_{i},\boldsymbol{f}_{i}\}

(i=1,2)
be two solutions to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) correspo-nding to the input data
(\boldsymbol{v}_{0i},\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{i},\nabla\psi_{i},g_{i})\ (i=1,2)
. Then, there exists a constant
C
such that

(1)

for
d=2
and
r\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{-1})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}-\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{01}-\boldsymbol{v}_{02}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{0}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|(g_{1}-g_{2})_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}

(1.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|\nabla(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})-\mu\Delta(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}\big),

(2)

for
d=3
and
r\geq 3

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\big(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{-1}+{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\big)}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}-\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{01}-\boldsymbol{v}_{02}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{0}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|(g_{1}-g_{2})_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}}}\right.

(1.14)

\displaystyle\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.+\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}+\|\nabla(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})-\mu\Delta(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{2}{r+1}}}}\right),

where
C
depends on the input data,
\mu,\alpha,\beta,r
and
T
.

Theorem
1.1
immediately implies the uniqueness in determining the solutions
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), that is, the uniqueness of solutions can be derived from (

(
1
)

) and (

(
2
)

).

The rest of the paper is structured as follows: In the next section, we first discuss the function spaces and some important inequalities. After defining the function spaces, we provide the relation between the solvability of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) and the equivalent nonlinear operator equation (Theorem
2.1
). We derive a number of
a-priori
estimates and some regularity results for the solutions of the CBF equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) required to investigate the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) in Section
3
. We prove the first part of our main result (Theorem
1.1

(i)
) in Section
4
, by first proving the existence of a solution to the equivalent operator equation by using the Tikhonov fixed point theorem. We have provided a concrete example also to validate our claims in the same section (Example
4.5
). The second part of Theorem
1.1

(ii)
is proved in Section
5
by establishing the uniqueness and stability of the solution to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
). We provide the proof of Theorem
2.1
in Appendix
A
. Moreover, we deduce some useful energy estimates that are required to investigate the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) in Appendix
B
.

2.
Mathematical Formulation

This section begins by introducing the function spaces and standard notations that will be used throughout the paper. We consider the problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) on a
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/(L\mathbb{Z})^{d},\ L>0
. Then, we provide a mapping which transforms the original inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) into an equivalent nonlinear operator equation of second kind (
2.7
) and verify their equivalence (Theorem
2.1
).

2.1.
Function spaces

Let
{\mathrm{C}}_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of all infinitely differentiable functions (
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued) such that
\boldsymbol{v}(x+{L}e_{i})=\boldsymbol{v}(x),
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
i=1,\ldots,d
, where
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\}
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As mentioned in
[
19
]
, the absorption term
\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
does not preserve zero mean-value constraints on the functions, that is,
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\boldsymbol{v}(x),\/\mathrm{d}\/x=0
, as is the case with the NSE. Therefore, we cannot apply the well-known Poincaré inequality
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, and we must consider the full
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-norm.

The Sobolev space
{\mathbb{H}}_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):={\mathrm{H}}_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the completion of
{\mathrm{C}}_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to the
\mathbb{H}^{s}
-norm

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{{\mathbb{H}}_{p}^{s}}:=\bigg(\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq s}\|\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

The Sobolev space of periodic functions
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the same as (
[
43
]
)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\boldsymbol{y}:\boldsymbol{y}(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x},\ \overline{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}=\boldsymbol{y}_{-k},\ \|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}_{f}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+|k|^{2s})|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty}}\right\}.

From
[
43
, Proposition 5.38]
, we infer that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{p}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{f}}
are equivalent.
Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathrm{C}}_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle:=\text{the closure of }\ \mathcal{V}\ \text{ in the Lebesgue space }\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}

\displaystyle:=\text{the closure of }\ \mathcal{V}\ \text{ in the Sobolev space }\ \mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}

\displaystyle:=\text{the closure of }\ \mathcal{V}\ \text{ in the Lebesgue space }\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

for
p\in(2,\infty]
. Then, we characterize the spaces
\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{V}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}
with the norms

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,\quad\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}+|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/x,\quad\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}^{p}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{p}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,\ \

for
p\in(2,\infty),
and

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|,

respectively.

For the Fourier expansion
\boldsymbol{v}(x)=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\boldsymbol{v}_{k},
we have by using Parseval’s identity

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}|^{2}\ \text{and}\ \|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=(2\pi)^{4}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{4}|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}|^{2}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+|k|^{4})|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}|^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Moreover, by the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\geq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
and hence it is immediate that
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\simeq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}
.

In the case of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(see
[
44
]
), every vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
can be uniquely decomposed into

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{w}+\nabla q,

where
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\nabla q\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
(Helmholtz-Weyl or Helmholtz-Hodge decomposition). We can express this decomposition as

{\mathbb{L}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\mathbb{H}\oplus\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

where
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathbb{H}
in
{\mathbb{L}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, which consists of gradients of scalar functions belonging
\mathbb{H}^{1}
.

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)
denote the inner product in the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
represent the induced duality between the spaces
\mathbb{V}
and its dual
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
as well as
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
and its dual
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}
, where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. Note that
\mathbb{H}
can be identified with its dual
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
.
Wherever needed, we assume that
p_{0}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),
where
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=0}}\right\}
.
The norm in the space
\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{0}
, that is,
\|g\|_{0}:=\sup\limits_{(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T]}|g(x,t)|
.

2.1.1.
Important inequalities

In the sequel,
C
denotes a generic constant which may take different values at different places. The following Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s and Agmon’s inequa-lities are used repeatedly in the paper:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}}\right)}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}}\right)},\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}}\right)}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{d}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}}\right)},\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

where
2\leq p<\infty
for
d=2
and
2\leq p\leq 6
for
d=3
, and

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}+\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\right),\ \ \ \text{ for }d=2,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/4}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}}^{3/4}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{3/4}+\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{3/4}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{3}{4}}}}\right),\ \ \ \text{ for }d=3,

for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In 2D, the well-known Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality, that is,
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and the inequality
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\leq C\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},
for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
will also be used (see Lemma 2.6,
[
17
]
).

2.1.2.
Nonlinear operator

Let us now consider the operator
\mathcal{C}:\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
defined by
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}):=|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
. It can be easily seen that
\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}
. Furthermore, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
, the map is Gateaux differentiable with the Gateaux derivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{w}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\boldsymbol{w},&\text{ for }r=1,\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{w}+(r-1)\frac{\boldsymbol{v}}{|\boldsymbol{v}|^{3-r}}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}),&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{v}\neq\mathbf{0},\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{v}=\mathbf{0},\end{array}}}\right.&\text{ for }1<r<3,\\
|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{w}+(r-1)\boldsymbol{v}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-3}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}),&\text{ for }r\geq 3,\end{array}}}\right.

for all
\boldsymbol{w}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
. For
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
, it can be easily verified that

(2.5)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}|\boldsymbol{w}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+(r-1)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-3}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\geq 0,

for
r\geq 1
(note that for the case
1<r<3
, (
2.5
) holds, since in that case the second integral becomes
\int_{\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:\boldsymbol{v}(x)\neq 0\}}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{3-r}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\geq 0
). For
r\geq 3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{w}\otimes\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=(r-1)\big\{|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-3}[(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\boldsymbol{w}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}]\big\}

\displaystyle\quad+(r-1)(r-3)\big[|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-5}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\boldsymbol{v}\big],

for all
\boldsymbol{v}\neq\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
and is zero for
\boldsymbol{v}=\mathbf{0}
.

2.2.
Equivalent formulation

For the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), let us provide an equivalent formulation as a nonlinear operator equation. Consider
\mathcal{D}
to be a subset of
\mathbb{H}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}\ :\ \|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq M}}\right\},

where
M
is a positive constant, which will be specified later. We define a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{H}
by

(2.6)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{f})(x):=\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x,T),\ \text{ for }\ x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
\boldsymbol{v}(x,T)
is the function entering the set
(\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p)
and solving the direct problem (
1.1
)-(
1.4
). Then, for
\boldsymbol{f}
, we analyze the following nonlinear operator equation of the second kind:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}=\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{f}:=\frac{1}{g(x,T)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{f}+(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}\right),

over the space
\mathcal{D}
.

The connection between the solvability of the nonlinear operator equation of the second kind (
2.7
) and the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) is shown in the following theorem:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\ (d=2,3)
be a torus,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H},\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathbb{V},\ \nabla\psi\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
g,g_{t}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
satisfy the assumption (
1.8
), and let the conditions (
1.10
)-(
1.12
), and
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
are sufficiently large (see Remark
4.2
), be satisfied. Then, the operator equation (
2.7
) has a solution lying within
\mathcal{D}
if and only if the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) has a solution.

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

For
d=2,3
and
r\in(3,\infty)
, the diffusion term
-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}
and the nonlinear damping term
\beta|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}
dominate the convective term
(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}
, and it helps us to obtain the solvability of the operator equation (
2.7
) without any restriction on the data (cf. (
1.11
)). But in the case of
d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
, such a domination seems to be not possible and we have to enforce a restriction on the data (see (
1.10
)).

3.
Some Useful Energy Estimates

Here we derive a number of useful
a-priori
energy estimates for the solutions of the CBF equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) required to investigate the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) (see Appendix
B
for a discussion on well-posedness and energy estimates).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\nabla p(\cdot))
be the unique solution of the CBF equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
. Then, for
r\geq 1
, the following estimate holds:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

and

\displaystyle\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\beta\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(3.2)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+t\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right).

Moreover, there exists a time
t_{1}\in\big(\frac{T}{8},\frac{2T}{8}\big)
such that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\},

holds.

Proof.

Let us take the inner product with
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
to the equation (
1.1
) and use the fact that
\langle(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0
and
\langle\nabla p,\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

(3.5)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))\leq\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where we have performed the integration by parts over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and used Hölder’s inequality. Thus, it is immediate that

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

The variation of constants formula yields for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq e^{-\alpha t}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1-e^{-\alpha t}}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

and the estimate (
3.1
) follows. Integrating (
3
) from
0
to
t
and then using (
3.1
), one can deduce (
3.1
).

Let us now prove (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). From Remark
B.8
, we have

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}\big([T/8,T];\mathbb{V}\big).

By the mean value theorem, there exists a time
t_{1}\in\big(\frac{T}{8},\frac{2T}{8}\big)
such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\frac{2T}{8}-\frac{T}{8}}\int_{T/8}^{2T/8}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t=\frac{8}{T}\int_{0}^{2T/8}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

which leads to (
3.3
) by using (
3.1
) and Young’s inequality. Using the similar arguments as above, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}=\frac{8}{T}\int_{T/8}^{2T/8}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\leq\frac{8}{T}\int_{0}^{2T/8}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

and (
3.4
) follows from (
3.1
) and Young’s inequality.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\nabla p(\cdot))
be the unique solution of the CBF equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
. Let
t_{1}\in\big(\frac{T}{8},\frac{2T}{8}\big),
be the same time obtained in Lemma
3.1
. Then, for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
,

(i)

for
d=2
and
r\in[1,3],
we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{1},T]}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right]+\frac{1}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

and

(3.7)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right]+\frac{t-t_{1}}{\mu^{2}}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(ii)

for
d=2,3
and
r>3
with
\eta=\frac{2(r-3)}{\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-3}}<2\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t]}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\big(2\alpha-\eta\big)\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\beta\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(3.8)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right]+\frac{2(t-t_{1})}{\mu}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(iii)

for
d=r=3
with
\beta\mu>1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t]}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\bigg(\beta-\frac{1}{\mu}\bigg)\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(t)||\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(3.9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right]+\frac{2(t-t_{1})}{\mu}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Proof.

Taking the inner product with
-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
in (
1.1
) and integrating the resulting equation over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(3.10)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(t))-\langle(\boldsymbol{v}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}(t),-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(t)\rangle,

for a.e.
t\in[t_{1},T]
. We observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(x))\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x+4\bigg(\frac{r-1}{(r+1)^{2}}\bigg)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}|^{r}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\frac{r-1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-3}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Moreover, we have the following result (see Lemma 2.1,
[
36
]
):

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\cdot(-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(x))\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq r\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Using an integration by parts over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and then using the divergence free condition on the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
(that is,
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0
) and periodicity of the pressure
p
in the resulting equation, we obtain

(3.11)

\displaystyle\langle\nabla p,-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0.

Case I:

d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
.

We estimate the term
|(\boldsymbol{f}g,-\Delta\boldsymbol{v})|
using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities as

(3.12)

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{f}g,-\Delta\boldsymbol{v})|

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\mu}\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

For
d=2
, over a torus, we know that (see Lemma 3.1,
[
47
,
48
]
),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\langle(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v},-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0.

Substituting the estimates (
3.11
)-(
3.13
) in (
3
), we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\alpha\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

An application of the variation of constants formula yields for all
t\in[t_{1},T]

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha(t-t_{1})}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1-e^{-2\alpha(t-t_{1})}}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

and (
3.6
) follows by using (
3.3
). Substituting (
3.11
)-(
3.13
) in (
3
), and integrating the resulting estimate from
t_{1}
to
t
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\beta\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\alpha\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{t-t_{1}}{\mu}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
and we obtain (

) by using the estimate (
3.3
).

Case II:

d=2,3
and

.

Again using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we observe the following estimate for

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right)^{\frac{r-3}{r-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta\mu}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\frac{r-3}{r-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

) results to

, where

)

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\nabla p(\cdot))
be the unique solution of the CBF equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
. Let
t_{1}\in\big(\frac{T}{8},\frac{2T}{8}\big)
be the time obtained in Lemma
3.1
. Then, for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
,

(i)

for
d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{K_{11}}{T}+K_{12}\bigg)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(\frac{3T}{4}+K_{13}\bigg)\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg]^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]^{\frac{4}{3}}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{4}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{3T}{8\mu^{2}}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg],

where

(3.21)

\displaystyle K_{11}=4+\frac{8}{r+1},\ \ \ K_{12}=\frac{5\alpha}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{r+1},\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ K_{13}=\frac{6}{\alpha}+\frac{8}{(r+1)\alpha},

(ii)

for
d=2,3
and
r>3
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{K_{21}}{T}+K_{22}T+K_{23}\bigg)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(K_{24}T+K_{25}\bigg)\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3T}{8\alpha}\|g_{t}\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where

(3.23)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\gamma=\frac{r-3}{r-1}\bigg(\frac{2}{\beta(r-1)}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{r-3}},\ \ \ K_{21}=4+\frac{4}{\mu}+\frac{8}{r+1},\ \ \ K_{22}=\frac{3\alpha}{4}+\frac{3\alpha\gamma}{32\mu},\\
&K_{23}=\bigg(\frac{7}{2}+\frac{1}{r+1}+\frac{1}{2\mu}\bigg)\alpha+\frac{\gamma}{2\mu},\ \ \ K_{24}=\frac{3}{4\alpha}+\frac{3}{4\mu}+\frac{3\gamma}{4\mu\alpha},\\
&\text{and}\ \ \ \ \ K_{25}=\bigg(7+\frac{4}{r+1}+\frac{2}{\mu}\bigg)\frac{2}{\alpha},\end{aligned}}}\right.

(iii)

for
d=r=3
with
\beta\mu>1
, we have

\displaystyle\ \ \int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{K_{31}}{T}+K_{32}T+K_{33}\bigg)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(K_{34}T+K_{35}\bigg)\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3T}{8\alpha}\|g_{t}\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(3.25)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{31}=6+\frac{2}{\beta\mu-1},\ \ \ K_{32}=\frac{3\alpha}{4},\ \ \ K_{33}=\bigg({15}+\frac{1}{(\beta\mu-1)}\bigg)\frac{\alpha}{4},\\
&K_{34}=\frac{3}{4\alpha}+\frac{3}{8(\beta\mu-1)},\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ K_{35}=\bigg(8+\frac{1}{(\beta\mu-1)}\bigg)\frac{2}{\alpha}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Moreover, there exists a time
t_{2}\in\big(\frac{2T}{8},\frac{3T}{8}\big)
such that

\displaystyle\quad+C\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg]^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]^{\frac{4}{3}}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{4}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{3T}{8\mu^{2}}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]\bigg],

(3.28)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3T}{8\alpha}\|g_{t}\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg\}.

Proof.

Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(\cdot)
in (

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{r+1}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t))-((\boldsymbol{v}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)),

where we have used the fact that

. For

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t)}}\right)=\frac{1}{r+1}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}.

Case I:

d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
.
We estimate the term

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{f}g,\boldsymbol{v}_{t})|\leq\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Making use of Hölder’s, Sobolev’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate the term

\displaystyle|((\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{6}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{1}{3}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(3.31)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{4}{3}}\|\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Substituting the estimates (

) and (

), and integrating the resulting estimate from

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(3T/8)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(3T/8)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2\beta}{r+1}\|\boldsymbol{v}(3T/8)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2\beta}{r+1}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2\beta}{r+1}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

for all

.
Thus, by using the estimates (

Case II:

.

We have

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(t))=\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))-(\boldsymbol{f}g_{t}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)),

An estimate similar to (

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2\beta}{r+1}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}+\alpha\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\frac{3T}{8}-t_{1}}{\alpha}\|g_{t}\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2(\boldsymbol{f}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))-2(\boldsymbol{f}g(t_{1}),\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1}))

\displaystyle\quad+\beta\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\gamma\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(\alpha+\frac{3\alpha T}{8}\bigg)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2\beta}{r+1}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}+\frac{3T}{8\alpha}\|g_{t}\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+4\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{4}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta\int_{t_{1}}^{\frac{3T}{8}}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\gamma}{\mu}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{3T}{8}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{3T}{8}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg)

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

(i)

The energy estimates mentioned in Section
3
and Appendix
B
allow us to prove the existence and uniqueness of a solution to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) as well as to establish the stability of the solution. To show the existence of a solution to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), we use Theorem
2.1
to prove that the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{B}
has a fixed point in
\mathcal{D}
, which follows from an application of the well-known Tikhonov fixed point theorem.

Subsequently, arguments for the existence of the solution of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) are based on the works
[
15
,
41
]
, where the existence of the solution of the inverse problem for 2D NSE has been examined by exploiting Tikhonov’s fixed point theorem.

Theorem 4.1

(Tikhonov’s fixed point theorem,
[
38
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a non-empty bounded closed convex subset of a separable reflexive Banach space
\mathbb{X}
and let
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
be a weakly continuous mapping (that is, if
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\in\mathcal{D},\ \boldsymbol{v}_{n}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}
weakly in
\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{v}
weakly in
\mathbb{X}
as well). Then,
\mathcal{B}
has at least one fixed point in
\mathcal{D}
.

4.1.
Existence

We commence proving Theorem
1.1
(i) by ensuring that the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{B}
defined in (
2.7
) fulfills all the assumptions given in Theorem
4.1
. The following remark is crucial in this work and the subsequent lemma demonstrates that the operator
\mathcal{B}
maps
\mathcal{D}
into itself.

Remark 4.2
.

Let us specify the choice of
M
for defining the closed ball
\mathcal{D}
. For
d=2,3
and
r>3
, we define
M
by

\displaystyle M=M(\alpha,\mu)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\bigg(\frac{8K_{21}}{T^{2}}+8K_{22}+\frac{8K_{23}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}}{g_{T}-\bigg\{\bigg(8K_{24}+\frac{8K_{25}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha-\eta^{*})}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\bigg\}},

where
K_{2i},\ i=1,\ldots,5,
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.23
)), and
\eta^{*}=\frac{r-3}{\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-3}}
, and
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
is taken sufficiently large such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle g_{T}>\bigg(8K_{24}+\frac{8K_{25}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha-\eta^{*})}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}.

Similarly, for
d=r=3
with
\beta\mu>1
, we can define
M
by

\displaystyle M=M(\alpha,\mu)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\bigg(\frac{8K_{31}}{T^{2}}+8K_{32}+\frac{8K_{33}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}}{g_{T}-\bigg\{\bigg(8K_{34}+\frac{8K_{35}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\bigg\}},

where
K_{3i},\ i=1,\ldots,5,
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.25
)), and
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
are taken sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle g_{T}>\bigg(8K_{34}+\frac{8K_{35}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}.

It is worth emphasizing that
M
is large if
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
are large, which indicates that the unknown part
\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot)
of the source could be large. A similar choice of
M
has to be made in the case of
d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
also (cf. (
4.1
) below).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H},\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathbb{V}
,
\nabla\psi\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
g,g_{t}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
satisfy assumption (
1.8
). If
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
are sufficiently large so that the conditions given in Remark
4.2
(cf. (
4.1
)) are satisfied, then the operator
\mathcal{B}
maps
\mathcal{D}
into itself.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}
. From (
2.7
), we have

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{1}{g_{T}}\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg\}.

Case I:

d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
.
At the final time
t=T
, from (

(
i
)

), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\bigg(\frac{K_{11}}{T}+K_{12}\bigg)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(\frac{3T}{4}+K_{13}\bigg)\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg\}^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg\}^{\frac{4}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\frac{4}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{3T}{8\mu^{2}}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg\}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{8}{T}+\frac{8}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\frac{T}{\alpha}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

where
K_{1i},\ i=1,2,3,
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.21
)).
Plugging the relation (
4.1
) in (
4.2
) gives

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{g_{T}}\bigg[\bigg[\bigg(\frac{K_{11}}{T}+K_{12}\bigg)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\bigg(\frac{3T}{4}+K_{13}\bigg)\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg\}^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\frac{4}{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{2\mu\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg\}^{\frac{4}{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\frac{4}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{3T}{8\mu^{2}}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg\}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\frac{8}{T}+\frac{8}{\mu^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{\alpha}{8}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right\}+\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\alpha}\|g\|_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\frac{T}{\alpha}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg]\leq M.

Case II:

d=2,3
and
r>3
.

At the final time
t=T
, from (

(

⁢
i
i

)

), we deduce

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{8K_{21}}{T^{2}}+8K_{22}+\frac{8K_{23}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\bigg(8K_{24}+\frac{8K_{25}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha-\eta^{*})}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

where
K_{2i},\ i=1,\ldots,5,
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.23
)) and
\gamma=\frac{r-3}{r-1}\bigg(\frac{2}{\beta(r-1)}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{r-3}}.

Substituting (
4.1
) in (
4.2
), we infer

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{g_{T}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{8K_{21}}{T^{2}}+8K_{22}+\frac{8K_{23}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\bigg(8K_{24}+\frac{8K_{25}}{T}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha-\eta^{*})}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg]\leq M.

with

.

in (

(

i
i

)

), from (

), we deduce

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\frac{3}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{t}\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg]

(4.7)

\displaystyle\leq M,

(see (

)) and this proves Lemma
4.3
.

The following lemma proves that there is a solution to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
hold true. Then,
\mathcal{B}
is weakly continuous from
\mathcal{D}
into itself.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{H}
is weakly continuous. Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequences of the solutions to the direct problem (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) corresponding to the external forcing
\{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}g\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. At the final time
t=T
, from (
1.1
), we have

(4.8)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})_{t}(T)=\boldsymbol{f}_{k}g(T)+\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}-(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}-\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}-\nabla\psi.

Let the sequence
\{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{D}
be such that
\boldsymbol{f}_{k}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{f}\text{ weakly in }\mathbb{H}
(that is,
(\boldsymbol{f}_{k}-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{w})\rightarrow 0\ \ \text{in}\ \ \mathbb{H}
-norm for all
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}
).
We know that the spaces
\mathbb{H},\mathbb{V}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
are reflexive.
Making use of the energy estimates (cf. (
B.1
), (
B.17
)) and the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, we can extract subsequences
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{k_{l}}\}
of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\}
, such that (for simplicity, we denote the index
k_{l}
by
k
)
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}\textrm{ weakly in
}\mathbb{H},
for all
t\in[0,T]
. From equation (
4.8
), for all
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\big|((\boldsymbol{v}_{k})_{t}(T),\boldsymbol{w})|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{\|g\|_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{k}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg\}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\infty.

Thus, it is immediate that (cf. (

(

⁢
i
i

)

) and Remark
B.8
below)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})_{t}(T)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(T)\textrm{ weakly in
}\mathbb{H}.

On passing the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
4.8
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{f}_{k}=(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})_{t}(T)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(T)=\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{f}\textrm{ weakly in }\mathbb{H}.

So, the operator
\mathcal{A}
is weakly continuous. Thus, the operator
\mathcal{B}
is weakly continuous.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1

(i)
.

From Lemma
4.4
, we know that the operator
\mathcal{B}
is weakly continuous. Hence,
\mathcal{B}
has a fixed point in
\mathcal{D}
. From Theorem
2.1
, we infer that the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) has a solution.
∎

Example 4.5
.

For
g\equiv 1
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, and

\displaystyle\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}<(1-8K_{24})^{\frac{1}{2}}M,

with
8K_{24}<1
, that is,
\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{\gamma}{\mu\alpha}+\frac{1}{\mu}<\frac{1}{6}
, then
the condition (
4.1
) becomes

\frac{1}{T}\leq\frac{\big(1-8K_{24}\big)M^{2}-\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}{8M^{2}K_{25}},

where
\ K_{24}
and
K_{25}
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.23
)).
Therefore, for any

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{8M^{2}K_{25}}{\big(1-8K_{24}\big)M^{2}-\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}},

and under the condition (
1.11
) for
d=2,3
and
r\in(3,\infty)
, there exists a solution of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), for
d=2,3
and
r\in(3,\infty)
.

Similarly, for
d=r=3
,
g\equiv 1
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, and

\displaystyle\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}<(1-8K_{34})^{\frac{1}{2}}M,

with
8K_{34}<1
, that is,
\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2(\beta\mu-1)}<\frac{1}{6}
, the condition (
4.1
) becomes

\frac{1}{T}\leq\frac{\big(1-8K_{34}\big)M^{2}-\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}{8M^{2}K_{35}}

where
K_{34}
and
K_{35}
are defined in Lemma
3.3
(see (
3.25
)).
Therefore, for any

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{8M^{2}K_{35}}{\big(1-8K_{34}\big)M^{2}-\big\|(\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\nabla\psi-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\beta|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}},

there exists a solution of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
), for
d=r=3
.

5.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

(ii)

In the previous section, we have proved the existence of a solution
\{\boldsymbol{v},\nabla p,\boldsymbol{f}\}
to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
). To obtain results on the uniqueness and stability, we first provide some supporting lemmas. Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{i},\nabla p_{i},\boldsymbol{f}_{i}\}

(i=1,2)
be the solutions of the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) corresponding to the given data
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{0i},\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{i},\nabla\psi_{i},g_{i}\}

(i=1,2)
and set

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \boldsymbol{v}:=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \nabla p:=\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2}),\ \ \ \ \ \ \boldsymbol{f}:=\boldsymbol{f}_{1}-\boldsymbol{f}_{2},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{v}_{01}-\boldsymbol{v}_{02},\ \ \ \boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}:=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2},\ \ \ \nabla\psi:=\nabla(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}),\ \ \ g:=g_{1}-g_{2}.

Later discussions of the uniqueness and stability of the solution to the inverse problem (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) are based on the paper
[
15
]
, in which the authors demonstrate the uniqueness and stability of the solution to the inverse problem for NSE in two dimensions.

In this section, we assume that
\alpha,\beta
and
\mu
are sufficiently large so that the conditions given in Remark
4.2
(cf. (
4.1
)) are satisfied.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{0i}\in\mathbb{H}
,
g_{i},(g_{i})_{t}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
satisfy assumption (
1.8
) and
\boldsymbol{f}_{i}\in\mathbb{H}
, for
i=1,2
. For
r\geq 1,\ \text{in}\ 2D
, and for
r\geq 3,\ \text{in}\ 3D
(
\beta\mu\geq 1
for
r=3
), the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(5.1)

\displaystyle\leq C\big\{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|g\|_{0}^{2}\big\}.

Moreover, there exists a time
t_{3}\in\big(\frac{3T}{8},\frac{4T}{8}\big)
such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t_{3})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq C\big\{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|g\|_{0}^{2}\big\}

holds and
C
depends on the input data,
\mu,\alpha,\beta,r
and
T
.

Proof.

Subtracting the equations for
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{i},\nabla p_{i},\boldsymbol{f}_{i}\}\ (i=1,2)
, we find

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+\alpha\boldsymbol{v}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}}}\right)+\nabla p=\boldsymbol{f}g_{1}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}g,

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
in
\mathbb{H}
. Multiplying both sides of (
5
) by
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
and integrating the resulting equation over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big((\boldsymbol{f}g_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}g(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)-\big((\boldsymbol{v}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)-|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)}}\right).

We estimate
|(\boldsymbol{f}g_{1}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}g,\boldsymbol{v})|
using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities as

(5.5)

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{f}g_{1}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}g,\boldsymbol{v})|\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\big(\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|g_{1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|g\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\big)^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

For
r\geq 1
, we have (see Sec. 2.4,
[
35
,
36
]
)

\displaystyle\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}}}\right)

(5.6)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\big\||\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

It is important to note that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(x)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(x)|^{r-1}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(x)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(x)|^{r-1}+|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(x)|^{r-1}}}\right)|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(x)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\big\||\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\big\||\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right).

From the above inequality, we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\frac{2^{2-r}\beta}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\leq\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Gathering both the estimates (
5
) and (
5.7
), we obtain

(5.8)

\displaystyle\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}}}\right)\geq\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}.

Case I:

d=2
and
r\in[1,3]
.

Applying Hölder’s, Ladyzhenskaya’s and Young’s inequalities, one gets

\displaystyle|((\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{v})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Plugging the estimates (
5.5
), (
5.8
) and (
5
) in (
5
), and integrating the resulting estimate from
0
to
t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(5.10)

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{t}{\alpha}\big(\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|g_{1}\|_{0}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|g\|_{0}\big)^{2}+\frac{2}{\mu}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. An application of Gronwall’s inequality in (
5
) gives
